The Wild Stories Behind Trapped Under Ice’s 2007 Demo

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Almost 20 years ago, Baltimore’s Trapped Under Ice released their first demo — a record that would sow the seeds, in many ways, of modern hardcore. After all, it launched the career of one of the genre’s most dynamic frontmen, Justice Tripp of Angel Du$t, and later featured Turnstile‘s Brendan Yates on drums.

Now, the story behind the creation of that demo has finally been told — via the upcoming book “Intent to Distribute: Twenty Years of Flatspot Records,” out Feb. 5, 2027. The book, written by journalist Fred Pessaro, contains exclusive photos, artwork, and interviews with bands that the label launched, including Tripp, Backtrack, Terror, Speed, End It, and Scowl. Rolling Stone has the first excerpt, about TUI’s demo, below.

“When you spend so much time doing something, you’re usually focused on what’s next. You’re putting out the next record, planning the next campaign, working on the next show,” says label co-founder Ricky Singh. “You don’t always stop to look at everything that’s happened along the way. Making this book gave us an opportunity to do that. It’s a collection of people, bands, friendships and experiences that have shaped Flatspot over the last 20 years.”

Adds co-founder Che Figueroa-Rodriguez: “Throughout the last 20 years there has been a lot of blood and sweat and the driving force that carried us through every era of the label was that same passion we had when we started.”

That passion will be on full display at an anniversary show on Feb. 6 at New York’s Webster Hall, featuring performances by Trapped Under Ice, Breakdown, End It, Raw Brigade, Iron Mind, and Frothing Mad.

The Breakdown and Outburst demos in ’87. Beyond’s Dew it! and Turning Point’s demo in ’88. Merauder’s ’93 demo. All signs point toward Trapped Under Ice’s 2007 demo being named in the same breath as some of these legendary early efforts. Depending on the era, it’s quite possible that the record known as FSR02 could go down as the single most important hardcore demo of all time. Flatspot Records proudly released this five-song monster on vinyl after witnessing its creation in Joe Mitra’s basement so many years ago, and it has remained a crucial part of the catalog ever since. The record, initially paid for in stolen goods and later pressed with drug money, proved to be not only a major landmark for hardcore in the Mid-Atlantic, but for the genre writ large, causing a monolithic shift in sound and style. Editor’s picks The 250 Greatest Albums of the 21st Century So Far The 100 Best TV Episodes of All Time The 500 Greatest Albums of All Time 100 Best Movies of the 21st Century

No one, especially TUI vocalist Justice Tripp and Flatspot founder Che Figueroa, could have predicted the sea change that these songs would put into motion. But now with hindsight, it’s hard to argue the depth and breadth of influence caused by this gang of kids from one of the most dangerous cities in America — the waves in the water are still being felt. We sat down with Tripp to discuss what circumstances surrounded the demo and just how a bunch of “bad kids” ended up creating such seminal work. What follows is the story of Trapped Under Ice’s early days and everything that came with it, from rodent-infested homes to cocaine bricks to fights with tasers.

Trapped Under Ice was created from the ashes of Nick Fury. What was the genesis of the band? Nick Fury’s big creative impression was that we were a teenage straight edge band, but we were all reaching the age of 20 and our interests shifted. So me, Sam [Trapkin], and Ben [Esparza] decided to try something different and we were set on doing heavier music. The creative ideas came out of lifting sessions — me and Sam would lift in his hot garage and we only had one CD for the player: State of the World Address by Biohazard. I think we decided that the band should sound like us in our underwear, sweating, listening to State of the World Address.  Related Content Remix Albums Are Helping Pop Acts Live in the Moment Longer — And Feel Less Alone Turnstile Raise Over $250,000 for Charity at Massive Free Washington D.C. Show Turnstile Announce Free Washington Monument Show for Charity ‘SNL’: Watch Turnstile Perform ‘Birds,’ Medley From Album ‘Never Enough’

As far as influence, I hear classic NYHC like Breakdown, Killing Time, Crown of Thornz…Breakdown and Crown of Thornz, yeah. Madball was a subtle influence, so I would say maybe some of that Killing Time is through their influence on Madball. We always get Outburst, too, but I think when people think of them, they’re hearing Breakdown or the similarities between the two. I don’t think I even heard Outburst until we put out the TUI demo.

How did you arrive at the band name? Does it come from the classic Metallica song?It was Sam’s idea and at the time, it wasn’t consciously because of a Metallica song. We didn’t know that was the source of inspiration until the first time we saw it on a flyer and thought, “Makes sense, Sam’s a Metallica freak.” Also, I was writing lyrics before we had a name and there was a theme of “this is cold” and “that’s cold.” So when the name came about, it was like, “Oh, this fits the lyrics. We’re gonna run with that.”

Did “Stay Cold” exist before the name Trapped Under Ice?“Stay Cold” was an early song we wrote and actually, I do believe that was before we had named the band. We hadn’t recorded the song yet, but we had that song written for a while before we recorded it for the EP that came out later.

So you did the demo before you started playing shows?Yeah, we wanted to make sure we had music out before actually doing it live.

What were the details of recording the demo?  We recorded the demo in our friend’s basement — Joe Mitra’s Wired Wrists Studio. I remember we recorded and the snare drum sounded kind of crazy. So he was like, “I wanna try this,” and we’re all young and learning stuff. We didn’t really know what we were doing and were all experimenting in our own ways, and Joe ended up tweaking the snare drum. At the time, it didn’t bother me too much — I was like, “Oh, it’s weird.” I didn’t really know or care or think too much — it was just “is there a mosh part that makes me feel something?” After the vinyl came out, a friend of mine said, “I’m really sorry about that snare.” He thought I was upset about the drums and I was like, “Oh man, is something wrong with it?” As time goes on, you become more familiar with what things are supposed to sound like, but I think the snare is one of those things that makes it special. It has this alien sound where people are like, “Wow, these guys are from another planet.”

So you guys are recording the demo — clearly, you’re very young. How did you pay for the recording?We were definitely bad kids at that time and he probably charged us like $500 bucks to record the demo— probably $100 a song or whatever. We didn’t have that at the time, so we would just do shit that kids did — rob people and stuff. That’s not cool. I feel like at that time, we got into going to parties and if the vibe was cool, we were cool and then if it wasn’t, we were robbing people that were mean to us. And we basically funded recording that way: stole stuff from somebody’s house and the things we stole paid for our demo. You know, I’m surprised we never got locked up or killed or something.

How did “Soul Vice” come about? Was it originally an intro?  So when I wrote that song and riff, I remember thinking I wanted it to be an intro, so it’s structurally not like a verse and a chorus. I just wanted a cool riff that sounds like Breakdown, that fucking hits you with the mosh part.

Lyrically, I wanted to be kind of vague, but I think it’s like a cool summary of my family life, which was a little crazy, and I had just moved out — I was actually 17. I was learning to deal with living on my own and adulthood and having anxiety problems and then I was kind of thrown into a world where I didn’t have a support system. It was just me and my friends. I didn’t feel like I had anybody I could depend on. I was learning about loss, people dying around you, and just trying to process all these feelings at once. And now all these adult things are being thrown at me and I can’t deal with them all at once.

“Reality Unfolds” is such a good example of a certain thing that TUI does musically where you basically just tell Sam to write something fun. He always does this — overnight he’s like, “Hey, I wrote this last night,” and it’s what “Reality Unfolds” became.

Did Sam come up with some of the vocal cadences or did you do that?So, I do a lot of that but that’s one thing I like about Sam. He’s very producer-minded. A lot of times he can come up with a whole song musically and then I’ll riff on it but he’ll say, “I like when you did this,” or “Can you do that here? I don’t like this.” And he’ll give me fun challenges like, “I like this word, what if you put this word in the song somewhere?” I think he’s a really aware person.

I met Sam at a young age, and he’s from a different area and could observe me really well. He was really conscious of my voice, my accent and the way I talk. I don’t think I talk like a lot of people, especially before I started touring a lot — hearing my voice and accent in a microphone and being like, “Oh, I sound like Baltimore trash.” So he would play off of that and be like, “Hey man, can you say this word in the song?” I’d be like, “Why do you want me to say that word so bad?” And then you hear it — it’s because he thought it was funny I say it that way. He would always play off my accent and I think that’s a funny early TUI thing.

If you think about how those songs are structured, there are many parts where the music drops out and there will be a short vocal solo — it’s a very pop song approach. TUI’s got a lot of that. Where do you think that came from? Yeah, I love that, man. Those are moments that we were obsessed with, and at first we were like, “We should do that in every song.” Biohazard has a couple of those moments. A great example is on “Punishment” where he’s like, “I question, not me…” — that whole section. Another one is “March of the Damned” by Integrity where Dwid says, “Father! Take me back!”

That’s another thing that producer-minded Sam really emphasized — people love relating to music through the vocals. It’s what we all sing along to. I think there’s a more direct reference for that one: “Big Kiss Goodnight.” I showed him this riff I wanted to use and just like with “Reality Unfolds,” Sam came in the next day with “Big Kiss Goodnight” entirely written. My part was the simplest, dumbest riff on the whole record and he just made this crazy song. That includes the part where everything rings out and “you just say some cool-ass shit.”

How did the original cover for the demo come about? Was that originally intended to be a promo photo? Kind of? We knew we wanted a promo photo and there’s these bands we loved that were big scary guys. I think we went into it knowing that we weren’t big and scary. We couldn’t compete with the Fury of Five promo photo or the Box Cutter promo photo or Madball. We weren’t intimidating people and we took the photo as seriously as possible because we were referencing the stuff that we love. But the idea that we were acting like that was funny. It’s weird because I remember people were really upset like, “These guys think they’re so tough.” That’s the whole point — we’re not and that’s why it’s cool.

We just wanted it to be a cool statement of “here’s the promo photo, that’s the cover.” I remember Sam making the logo for that and I think it has icicles coming off it. We knew that was stupid — we’re not hiding around it. Self-aware is a good way to put it.

What’s the house that you guys are sitting in front of?It’s on Clinton Street in Highland Town at a house I lived in — me and a couple of friends. It was kind of the hangout spot. Some old Polish family that I knew used the house for storage and there was no hot water or heat or air conditioning. It was really sad. There were holes in the floor, so Baltimore-sized rats lived with us. The homies would come over and crash or go out and be debaucherous and that was where you all met after the fight breaks out or after the police get called. The neighborhood at the time was mainly El Salvadorans, who were really cool neighbors to have. I think they appreciated and supported our little active bad kid lifestyle.

What was the deal with the limited cover for the test press?We had the limited cover that was like a brick of cocaine and it said Trapped Under Ice written in lines of cocaine. When Che told me we could do that, I was like, “Oh, we can get like baking powder or something to do that with.” Che was like, “We could just use this,” and he pulled out a brick of cocaine. And then he did it, it looked cool and I was like, “That’s fucking sick — nobody else has that.”

One of your early members, Daniel Klipa, is the son of Daniel Higgs, the legendary artist, tattooer and member of Lungfish. How did you link with Klipa to form an early version of TUI? I think we recorded before he actually started practicing with us. He played the first shows that we did. Klipa was a local guy who grew up in a white trash neighborhood and just did white trash shit. The only hardcore he was aware of was Hatebreed and Icepick, so we were like, “We can convince him to do this by telling him it sounds like Icepick.” Every day with Klipa is like 20 stories. One day we went to practice and we called him and he said, “I moved to Florida.” He got kicked out of Florida for stealing purses, so the next week when it was time to practice, he was back in Maryland so he practiced with us. He’s a character, like out of a movie.

You talked about debaucherous evenings that started on Clinton Street. Give me an idea of what one of those might entail. There was a group of people that we were feuding with and I remember one night we found out that they were all squadded up somewhere, so we called all the boys. When Klipa got to my house, he walked across the street to the El Salvadoran family that we’re friends with and spoke to them in fluent Spanish, which none of us knew he could do. They gave him some beers and five bucks. He told them that he was really broke and needed gas to get somewhere to fight these guys, so they gave it to him. We drove to go fight these guys, found them and fought. So when we came back to the crib, the neighbors were like, “What happened? Did you beat their ass?” They were really excited about it.

Things definitely got a little out of hand and went further than anybody anticipated a couple times. Sometimes weapons got used, and at the time our weapon of choice was tasers. Everybody had tasers because we thought it was funny — if you tase somebody, sometimes they would pee themselves. And that was funny to be like, “Hey, we made this guy piss his pants and that’s how we won the fight.”

What were some of the early shows for TUI?  So I think the first show TUI played was a Ruiner show. Van Damage was a band in Pennsylvania and at the time, we would play with them a bit. Down to Nothing would invite us to play with them. And then a lot of local shows, like one a week for a long time. We were thinking we’re just gonna do a Baltimore show or two and then we kept getting asked to play out of the state, which was really exciting. When you’re a kid, you don’t have any intentions of leaving the state. I think Lemoyne, Pennsylvania, would have been our first out-of-state show. Brockton, Massachusetts, was also an early adopter of Trapped Under Ice.

It’s kind of also the time when Title Fight was becoming a real band. So we played a show in Wilkes-Barre with Title Fight. It was crazy. I remember thinking, people really like this band — they’re gonna do really well. They obviously exceeded whatever I thought they would do. Later, I became aware of a new band called Bad Seed that was just the members of Title Fight with the exception of another friend or two. And it was very much similar in influence to Trapped Under Ice. They’re all a little younger than us and from what they told me, they were like, ”Oh, you can make music like that and people are into it?” So they started Bad Seed. They were a band that we would play with a lot in the beginning, too.

For those early shows, did you have enough material to play in addition to the demo? Did you have any covers that you’d play? We had a cover of “Last Breath” by Hatebreed that we would do sometimes. We’d also do a little Metallica medley, “Street Fight” by Breakdown. I think that’s it. Our set was songs from the demo and then one of those.

Let’s talk about the Baltimore and D.C. scenes around the time of TUI’s ascension. What was it like then? Definitely much smaller, I’d imagine, but there were several bands from the area like Gut Instinct and Next Step Up. Between Virginia and Maryland, D.C., and Baltimore, there were so many different waves that were happening. Desperate Measures was really important to our friend circle. They were one of those bands that made it feel cool to be from Maryland, which wasn’t the coolest thing. I always say Slumlords was really cool because they were one of those first bands to be a Baltimore band. There were a couple bands from Baltimore, but nobody was wearing it like that. And I think that was big with TUI — we wanted to be a Baltimore hardcore band. We’re gonna put Baltimore on our merchandise and make it a cool thing because it really never was cool.

Honestly, I think Stout is the most important band in Baltimore hardcore history. So you got Gut Instinct, which is a little more traditional hardcore and influenced a lot of early hardcore bands. And then there was Next Step Up, which is more of the groovy thing that really influenced bands like Hatebreed. And then Stout was like, the next level and evolution of heavy music where it just sounds demonic and experimental on some level. But it’s like, hardcore dudes who really like Pentagram and are trying to see how hard they can make music. Definitely a big thing that TUI took from.

In hardcore, there are a lot of vocalists, but not many front people. Most vocalists lack that certain kind of “pied piper-ism” you possess. What are the qualities that make a good front person? Who were your pied pipers?I appreciate that, thank you. So, the obvious one that people caught before I did was Ezec from Crown of Thornz. He is such a character. He’s not bluffing one bit. That’s just who he is and you can’t turn it off. When I was a kid I was like, “I wanna be a unique individual,” and he’s one of the first people to turn me on to individuality in the context of heavy music. He doesn’t follow any of the rules.

I feel like there were two bands who were singing about their feelings, Crown of Thornz and American Nightmare — two bands from different generations. That’s one of the things people really loved about American Nightmare and made them a unique and very artistic band. I think neither of those bands were particularly focused on being heavy in the way that TUI was. I think our unique angle was to be like, “I got my feelings hurt by this girl“ or “I’m feeling abandoned by my father” on a song with a riff that sounds like Madball. But the ultimate lesson as a frontman in rock & roll music is Iggy Pop. That’s my number one. There are more obvious examples too, like Prince is really important to me.

When we first started playing, I remember Sam asking me about the way I moved onstage, shaking my hips and stuff. He was like, “I don’t know if that fits the vibe,” and I said, “That’s why it’s cool.” And to his credit, Sam might not always understand what I’m doing, but I’d explain myself and he’d be like, “Let’s run with it.” I’ve always done that — I always wanna get on stage and be flamboyant and a spectacle. I wanna move in a way that makes you uncomfortable. That’s not just hardcore music, that’s rock & roll music. I wanna engage and challenge people. Trending Stories Sade Are Not Attending Rock & Roll Hall of Fame Induction Ceremony The ‘You Can See Everything’ Interview You’ve Been Waiting For Bob Dylan’s Meeting With Pope Leo: People Fixated on the Hood, But the Hands Spoke Volumes Megan Thee Stallion and Daniel Radcliffe Reunite for Season Two of ‘Reggie Dinkins’

When the demo came out, it was kind of seismic — it really reverberated throughout the scene. When did you start to notice that the reaction was bigger than “pretty good for a local”?So Trash Talk is the first band to bring TUI out on a U.S. tour. We had just a demo out at the time, so it was kind of a risky thing for them. They were so successful and they stood up for us. They were like, “It’s gonna be this band. It doesn’t matter if they draw or not, this needs to happen.” And I remember they were beefing with their agent at the time. He was like, “I got this band and they have the numbers,” and they were like, ”We don’t give a fuck about the numbers.” It was really cool and humbling to have people I admired riding for us. So when we went out I thought, “We just fucked their tour up.” But every show was just insane.

When we played the Che Cafe in San Diego, Patrick Kitzel sent us 200 Stay Cold 7-inches — it had just come out. We sold all of them that night. The show was sold out and it was crazy. I don’t even know if that venue holds 200 people, but we sold 200 records. The guys who ended up becoming Take Offense were there and they were going wild. And then we played L.A. and people I looked up to who played in cool bands showed up, particularly like Jeremy Bolm from Touche Amore and Luis from Alpha & Omega. It’s still a lot of the same people I see when I go to L.A. and that’s insane.

Ebenezer Review: Johnny Depp Neither Helps Nor Hurts This Passionless Retelling

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Charles Dickens' 1843 novella "A Christmas Carol" is one of the greatest stories ever written. It's a tale that is both very much of its time as well as timeless, speaking to systemic issues of cold-hearted greed and inequality as well as the ability to change one's ways for the better. It's a malleable text that can be calibrated for all ages, just as it can be told with everything from a lighthearted touch to a more dour and sinister horror-centric one.

This versatility has led it to become one of the most commonly adapted stories of all time. To put it mildly, we are not lacking for versions of Dickens' "Christmas Carol." That doesn't mean further adaptations shouldn't be made, but rather that the artists behind them should have a unique take on the material; like Shakespeare, it's well known enough to not have to play completely straight.

Yet playing it straight seems to be the entire ethos for "Ebenezer," the new adaptation from director Ti West and starring Johnny Depp as Scrooge. If a "Christmas Carol" adaptation isn't taking a big swerve with either a singing Will Ferrell or Muppets, for example, the general assumption is that some renowned actor will give their take on Scrooge, as luminaries like Alastair Sim, Albert Finney, and George C. Scott have done. That seems to be what West and company are assuming with Depp in the role, and it's likely why the rest of the film around him isn't taking too many big swings. Yet it leaves "Ebenezer" feeling a little too plain on the one hand, and potentially unsavory where Depp's personal status is considered. It's a movie that seems to exist on Depp's shoulders for better or worse, a choice that leaves it being technically competent but ultimately confused.

If you already know "A Christmas Carol," then "Ebenezer" will not hold many surprises for you. Ti West and screenwriter Nathaniel Halpern treat Charles Dickens' novella in much the same way the Royal Shakespeare Company might treat "Hamlet," which is to say that they retain the bulk of Dickens' dialogue and structure (the film is even separated into five staves, as in the novella) while coloring outside the lines ever so often. After an original opening sequence detailing the literal end of the partnership between Marley (Ian McKellen) and Ebenezer Scrooge (Johnny Depp), the film chronicles the usual beats of the tale, from establishing Scrooge's miserly, calloused attitude to the gradual thawing of his soul via his haunting by three Christmas ghosts.

Although Paramount Pictures' ghastly marketing campaign promised that "Ebenezer" would present a Scrooge we'd never seen before and go places other adaptations hadn't dared, the true extent of any changes or embellishments to the Dickens tale lies in the way that certain very small supporting characters who purport to either be in service of the needy or in need themselves are revealed to be greedy con artists.

The choice does add a nice little layer of moral complexity to what is typically a straightforward story, yet West and company don't do all that much with it in the end. Given his extensive indie horror background, West does lend the proceedings a welcome macabre aesthetic. This is certainly the spookiest "Christmas Carol" since Robert Zemeckis' bizarre 2006 version. The three ghosts are especially ghastly and eerie, and the whole film has a Victorian London by way of '70s British films feel to it, like if Ronald Neame's "Scrooge" was mashed up with West's "The Innkeepers."

While much of "Ebenezer" is solid and skillfully crafted, it's all clearly in service of the character and the performer at its center. To say that Johnny Depp brings some baggage to the role is to make an understatement, for even setting aside the considerable issue of his offscreen life, his onscreen persona would be its own monolith to reckon with. Especially in this material, as it blatantly conjures up his past successes with director Tim Burton.

What made the Depp/Burton duo special was how the director was able to draw out some truly bizarre character choices from the actor while making sure the film around him fit the outrageous, heightened tone of the performance. Here in "Ebenezer," Depp appears to be shooting more for respectable prestige than crowd-pleasing outrageousness. He's not trying to pull a Jack Sparrow, in other words, and instead is letting the prosthetic makeup do much of the transformation for him.

With the exception of a small moment here and there, Depp's Scrooge is largely solid yet unremarkable. Which begs the question: why bother in the first place? It seems like the entirety of "Ebenezer" was arranged to allow Depp to chew scenery and showboat around, which the actor perversely refuses to do. It's possible that Depp now considers such an approach something he's no longer interested in, and it's not like he hasn't given grounded performances before, like his turn as John Dillinger in "Public Enemies," for example. Depp's performance as Scrooge simply doesn't feel fully realized. It's fine on paper, and even has flashes of depth, yet it ultimately is more calibrated than inspired.

I have a deep affection for "A Christmas Carol," and generally find something to like in all of its many adaptations. In this way, "Ebenezer" is no different. Ti West, Nathaniel Halpern, Depp and the rest haven't twisted the story beyond recognition, nor have they sullied it. Yet in playing things so generally safe, "Ebenezer" becomes a muted film.

West throws in a flourish here and there, like the great work with the three ghost characters or a moment where Scrooge is abducted through his bed that deliberately recalls Depp's cinematic debut in "A Nightmare on Elm Street." There's even an extended denouement to the tale which stands in contrast to most versions of "A Christmas Carol," where instead of rushing through Scrooge's change of heart, the film takes its time as the man makes his reparations.

Yet even that choice feels calculated in a meta sense to better present Johnny Depp, Movie Star, back to the public after his cancellation. After all, this story is famously one about a loathsome man who learns contrition and seeks to make amends, and while the film itself never breaks the fourth wall with this idea, it comes damn close. Even with that held back, it feels like "Ebenezer" is more a proving ground than impassioned adaptation, with West demonstrating he can do more than just indie horror, and Depp showing more of his restraint as a performer. What will happen to Depp the person is not for me to say, but he's going to have to work harder and take more risks than "Ebenezer" if he wants to give his screen career a second chance.

"Ebenezer" opens in theaters on November 13, 2026.

Bengals designate Andrei Iosivas, two others for return from IR

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

The Bengals will have three players back at practice on Thursday.

They announced that they have designated wide receiver Andrei Iosivas, cornerback Jalen Davis, and guard Brian Parker for return from injured reserve. All three players will be able to practice with the team for the next 21 days and they can return to the active roster at any point in that window.

Iosivas has been out since Week 2 with a thumb injury. He had three catches for 19 yards in the first two weeks of the season and had 69 catches over the previous two seasons.

Davis played in the first three games and has been out with a hamstring injury. He had five tackles, a tackle for loss and a pass defensed before his injury.

Parker was a sixth-round pick this year and landed on injured reserve ahead of Week 1.

Lions vs. Patriots matches up this year's two most aggressive head coaches on fourth downs

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

When the Lions meet the Patriots in Munich on Sunday, the fans in Germany are likely to see both teams go for it on fourth down.

That’s because Lions head coach Dan Campbell has long been the league’s most aggressive coach on fourth downs, and this year, Patriots coach Mike Vrabel is No. 2.

Coaches’ fourth down decisions are tracked for the Aggressiveness Index at FTN Fantasy, looking at how often coaches go for it compared to other coaches in the same situation. By that measurement, Campbell is first with a score of 1.45 (meaning he goes for it 45 percent more than an average NFL coach) and Vrabel is second with a score of 1.22.

The Aggressiveness Index is a more detailed way of tracking a coach’s fourth-down decision making than simply looking at the number of times a team has gone for it on fourth down. Some teams go for it often on fourth down not because the coach is aggressive, but because they happen to face a lot of fourth-and-shorts while trailing late in the fourth quarter. That’s not a particularly aggressive time to go for it.

What gives Campbell such a high score on the Aggressiveness Index is decisions like going for it on fourth down at his own 24-yard line with the score tied late in the fourth quarter against the Packers. That’s the kind of call that Campbell makes and other coaches don’t, which is why his Aggressiveness Index is so much higher than any other coach’s.

Campbell is at or near the top of the Aggressiveness Index every year. Last year he was second only to Mike Kafka, who was very aggressive on fourth downs during his seven-game stint as the Giants’ interim head coach. Perhaps unsurprisingly, after Kafka was not retained by the Giants, Campbell hired him to be the Lions’ passing game coordinator.

Vrabel has not been as aggressive as Campbell on fourth downs, but he has been more aggressive than the average NFL coach, both during his time with the Patriots and before that as head coach of the Titans. Both Vrabel and Campbell are former players with reputations for a blend of old-school toughness and a new-school understanding of the strategic advantages of aggressive fourth-down decision making. It’s a winning combination.

Taylor Swift to Receive Prestigious 2026 Sherry Lansing Leadership Award From Greta Gerwig at Women in Entertainment Gala

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

The award recognizes trailblazers and philanthropists in entertainment and media.

Taylor Swift will add another prestigious award to her shelf later this year when she receives the Sherry Lansing Leadership Awards during The Hollywood Reporter‘s annual Women in Entertainment breakfast gala in Los Angeles, which typically takes place in early December.

The magazine announced the news on Wednesday (Nov. 11), while revealing that Barbie director Greta Gerwig will present the award to Swift at the gala.

“Taylor Swift took on an industry that has long underestimated women and won on every front: artistically, commercially and on the question of who owns her work,” said THR editor-in-chief Maer Roshan. “She reclaimed her catalog, set new terms for the artists who follow her and became the most popular musician in the world while doing it — and helping scores of other artists along the way. We’re proud to honor her with the Sherry Lansing Leadership Award.”

The award named for the first woman to head a Hollywood Studio — Lansing is the former head CEO of Paramount Pictures — recognizes trailblazers and philanthropists in entertainment and media, with past recipients including Adele, Gwyneth Paltrow, Charlize Theron, Reese Witherspoon, Barbra Streisand, Meryl Streep and others.

“I am so pleased to recognize Taylor Swift with the award that bears my name,” Lansing told THR. “She has paved the way for women in music, demonstrating what is possible when a woman takes ownership of her work, her career and her future. Equally impressive is her generosity and commitment to giving back. She is an extraordinary talent and a remarkable role model, and I could not be more proud to see her receive this award.”

Swift and Gerwig are friends and have been spotted out together having dinner in New York and last October, the singer appeared on Late Night with Seth Meyers to promote her The Life of a Showgirl album, where she told a funny story about how then-boyfriend Travis Kelce mixed up Hugh Grant’s wife and Gerwig at an Eras Tour stop.

Troy Baker to Receive Legend Award at 2027 New York Game Awards (Gaming News Roundup)

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Troy Baker has been announced by the New York Videogames Critics Circle as the recipient of the Andrew Yoon Legend Award for the 2027 New York Game Awards.

With more than 400 roles across video games, television and film, Baker is one of the most well-known voice actors in the gaming industry, . He has brought to life a number of iconic video game characters, including his award-winning role as Indiana Jones for “Indiana Jones and the Great Circle.” Related Stories Daniel Dae Kim and 3AD Board North Korean Defector Documentary 'School for Defectors' (EXCLUSIVE)

“When we consider our upcoming Andrew Yoon Legend winners, we’re looking for individuals who’ve made a deep cultural impact on the video game landscape, and Troy Baker felt like the perfect addition,” said Harold Goldberg, NYVGCC co-founder and president. “Troy is such a force of nature within the games industry. We’ve recognized his voice-acting performances at previous New York Game Awards, and we are ecstatic to have him join our roster of Andrew Yoon Legend winners. Plus, our audiences are in for a treat with his musical performance after this year’s awards. You’ll only get to experience that moment in person at SVA Theatre!”

Following the awards, Baker will perform a musical set on Jan. 19 at the SVA Theatre in Manhattan. All ticket holders will be able to participate in the concert as a closer to the 16th annual New York Game Awards.

Proceeds from the Awards support NYVGCC’s Playing with Purpose writing program, which brings journalism and game narrative writing classes and workshops to New York City schools and shelters.

See more gaming news from the week in the roundup below.

“Star Trek” took over the Audrey Irmas Pavilion in Los Angeles Nov. 6 for the Magic: The Gathering Star Trek USS Gathering Experience.

The multi-level immersive experience celebrated the newest Universes Beyond set, “Magic: The Gathering | Star Trek,” which launches Friday. Among the night’s attendees were “Star Trek” actors Becky Lynch, Celia Rose Gooding and Gates McFadden.

In-game content platform Overwolf announced it paid creators over $300 million in 2025, totaling more than $800 million in total creator payouts.

Aiming to help modders earn a sustainable living by creating games, Overwolf is targeting $1 billion in annual payouts by 2030. The company supports games including “Minecraft,” which has 240,000 mods and 93 billion downloads, and “The Sims 4,” with 100,000 mods and 2.8 billion downloads.

Through its revenue-sharing model, creators are directly rewarded for their work. Those who opt to display ads receive a share of every dollar earned, while Premium Mod authors earn half of all revenue that is generated.

Netflix Playground has now added full episodes of popular children’s series to its library, allowing kids to play along as they watch.

Shows that are now available include “Doozie’s Doghouse,” “Paw Patrol,” “Peppa Pig,” “Gabby’s Dollhouse,” “Trash Trucks,” “Sesame Street,” “PJ Masks,” “Booba,” “Blippi’s Job Show,” “Red Fish Blue Fish” and “Horton.” New games were also added, including “Trash Truck,” “Sesame Street,” “Kpop Demon Hunters,” “Blippi,” “Number Blocks” and “My Very Hungry Caterpillar.”

Included in every Netflix membership, Netflix Playground has no ads or in-app purchases. In-app shows will rotate over time.

In anticipation of the long awaited release of “Grand Theft Auto 6,” Xbox is making the game available through Xbox Cloud Gaming, so the game will be playable on day one.

The game, which has become the most pre-ordered title of all time on the Microsoft Store, will be available on XBOX One and XBOX Series X|S, starting at 1 a.m. ET on Nov. 19.

You can pre-order “Grand Theft Auto VI” today on the Microsoft Store.

Irma P. Hall, Actress in ‘Soul Food’ and ‘The Ladykillers,’ Dies at 91

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

A Dallas schoolteacher for nearly three decades, she also appeared in 'A Family Thing,' 'Midnight in the Garden of Good and Evil' and 'Hap and Leonard.'

By Duane Byrge, Mike Barnes November 11, 2026 10:13am

Irma P. Hall, the late-blooming actress known for portraying no-nonsense matriarchs in Soul Food and the Coen brothers’ adaptation of The Ladykillers, has died. She was 91.

Hall died Tuesday, her family announced, calling her “a beloved actress, author, poet, educator and public speaker whose contributions touched countless lives throughout her remarkable career. Her legacy of talent, dedication and compassion will continue to inspire generations to come.”

A Texas native and longtime Chicago resident, Hall spent 27 years as a schoolteacher and worked as an actress for more than two decades before she first garnered national attention for playing the feisty Aunt T. in A Family Thing (1996), starring Robert Duvall and James Earl Jones as blood brothers. Related Stories Movies Stacy Keach, Who Played Badasses With the Best of Them, Dies at 85 TV JoBe Cerny, Actor, Director and Voice of the Pillsbury Doughboy, Dies at 78

She also was a preacher in Daniel Petrie’s Square Dance (1987), a nurse in Ron Howard’s Backdraft (1991), a voodoo priestess in Clint Eastwood’s Midnight in the Garden of Good and Evil (1997) and the domineering mother of Jamie Foxx’s character in Michael Mann’s Collateral (2004).

More recently, she made an impression as the wise, pie-making neighbor Meemaw on the SundanceTV series Hap and Leonard.

Hall is probably best known for portraying Mama Joe, whose Sunday night dinners were a family tradition, in Soul Food (1997) and then on the Showtime series that was based on the film written and directed by George Tillman Jr.

“People stop me in Chicago, I’m Big Mama,” Hall, who won an NAACP Image Award for her work in the movie, said in a 2008 interview. “Down in the Loop, they’ll see me across the street: ‘Hey, Big Mama, how you doing? Can I have a hug?’ ‘Yeah, baby, come on.’ I get lots of hugs and things.”

Hall received a special jury award at the Cannes Film Festival for her performance as Marva Munson, the cantankerous, churchgoing woman in Mississippi who rents a room to the crook Goldthwait Higginson Dorr III (Tom Hanks), in The Ladykillers (2004).

Irma Dolores Player Hall was born on June 3, 1935, in Beaumont, Texas and raised in Chicago. Her father was a saxophonist who played with big bands, and her mother was a housekeeper.

She graduated from Texas College in Tyler, Texas, then moved to Dallas to become an English and foreign-language teacher. During breaks, she also worked as a reporter for The Dallas Express, a weekly newspaper that focused on African-Americans.

While a teacher, Hall also co-founded The Dallas Minority Repertory Theatre, where she served as an administrator and performed. At age 36, while reciting a poem during a production, she was discovered and cast in Book of Numbers, a 1973 film for Embassy Pictures. Hall ventured out as an actress but continued to keep her day job.

In 1978, she appeared as the Ewings’ housekeeper, Tilly, on CBS’ Dallas and in the next two years on telefilms featuring the Dallas Cowboys Cheerleaders. Finally, she quit teaching to work full-time as an actor.

She went on to be seen in other films including Toni Morrison’s Beloved (1998), Patch Adams (1998), A Slipping-Down Life (1999), Bad Company (2002), Tyler Perry’s Meet the Browns (2008) and Jayne Mansfield’s Car (2012).

In January 2004, Hall was involved in a serious car accident during a snowstorm in Chicago, and she required open-heart surgery to repair a punctured aorta. The next year, she was inducted into the Texas Film Hall of Fame in Austin, introduced by Joel and Ethan Coen.

“It’s surprising when people come up and they want my autograph,” she once said. “I’m still saying, ‘For what?’ When I was doing something important like teaching school, nobody came to ask for my autograph.”

39 Award-Winning Photos From the 1839 Awards Photographers of the Year Contest

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

39 Award-Winning Photos From the 1839 Awards Photographers of the Year Contest

The 1839 Awards has announced the winners of its annual Photographer of the Year contest, celebrating exceptional, creative photography across multiple categories. Photographers from nearly 80 different countries entered the 2026 edition.

Giacomo Marchione was named the International Photographer of the Year 2026 for his series, The Invisible. Marchione’s five striking photos show underwater sea creatures, illuminated by light against a stark black background.

“At night, the sea doesn’t sleep,” Marchione says. “When the sun sets and the sea seems to fall silent, one of nature’s most extraordinary spectacles begins: the vertical migration of plankton. Millions of organisms rise from the depths to the surface in this upward migration.”

Marchione is a celebrated Italian underwater photographer as well as a practicing orthopedic surgeon. Marchione’s passion for underwater photography began in 1992 when he obtained his first scuba diving certification on vacation. He has photographed underwater all over the globe, including in the Maldives, Indonesia, Malaysia, and, of course, off the coast of Italy.

Marchione is not only the overall contest winner, but he also won the Underwater category.

On the Non-Professional side of the competition, photographer Dammy Twitch took top honors for his series, What If We Lived Among Gods. It’s a medium-format 120 film analog series exploring “myth, divinity, and everyday Nigerian life.”

Apampa Oluwadamilola, known professionally as Dammy Twitch, was born in 1995 and raised in Oyo State, Nigeria. Twitch relocated to Lagos and built his fine art photography, film, and music career. He was named to the Forbes 30 Under 30 list in 2022 and has received numerous accolades since, including the Rolling Stone Africa Mwamkom Vanguard Award for Visual Storytelling this year.

Twitch’s directorial debut, Call of My Life, has become one of the top five highest-grossing films ever in West Africa.

In total, there are many category winners across both Professional and Non-Professional sections. They can all be viewed on the 1839 Awards website. PetaPixel has selected a smattering of award-winning photos to share below, but everyone is highly encouraged to go view them all, organized by category, on the 1839 Awards website.

Image credits1839 Awards. Individual photographers are credited in the captions.

The Best Sci-Fi Movies of 2026 (So Far)

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Before Avengers: Doomsday and Dune: Part Three compete at the box office, Godzilla returns to remind everyone why he’s called the “King of the Monsters.”

Two of the biggest sci-fi movies of the year are upon us, when Avengers: Doomsday and Dune: Part Three both theaters on December 18. We’ll see if either multimillion-dollar blockbuster has the juice to compete with this year’s crop. Namely, Project Hail Mary, which kicked off the year on a very high note for sci-fi films.

Directed by Phil Lord and Christopher Miller, the Ryan Gosling-starring astronaut film that plays like Interstellar but if Matthew McConaughey had a talking rock for a friend was easily one of the best sci-fi film of the last five years. We also saw The End of Oak Street earlier this summer, and Steven Spielberg returned to the world of aliens with Disclosure Day. Plus, Godzilla Minus Zero just stomped into theaters to remind everyone just why they call him the “King of the Monsters” right before Dunesday.

Until then these are the best science-fiction movies of 2026.

The atomic monster returns to the screen following the success of Godzilla Minus One, as director Takashi Yamazaki sets up another stellar sequel for Godzilla to pulverize in his fight against King Gedorah. “In this series, Godzilla is a metaphor for war,” the filmmaker told Esquire. “What I wanted to do with this one is see how this family recovers and thrives after having suffered, and then see them survive another challenge.” Plus, leveling some cities with giant monster fights doesn’t hurt either.

The End of Oak Street is the best dinosaur film since Jurassic Park—and yes, I am including all the Jurassic Park sequels in that ranking. David Robert Mitchell’s sci-fi film about a neighborhood that is semi-transported back to prehistoric times stars Ewan McGregor and Anne Hathaway—in yet another great performance this year following The Odyssey—as the parents navigate their new Jumanji-like world.

Esquire’s Eric Francisco said it best when he described the beat-’em-up action flick based on the popular video game series as “incredibly stupid fun.” Noah Centineo and Andrew Koji star as friendly rivals Ryu and Ken alongside an oddball and ecentric cast including David Dastmalchian, Roman Reigns, Cody Rhodes, Andrew Schulz, Eric Andre, Orville Peck, 50 Cent, and Jason Momoa as the game’s various fighters. Still, amid all the chaos, it’s probably’s Callina Liang’s dedication to Chun-Li that shines through the brightest.

Following Barbarian and Weapons, Zach Cregger’s take on Resident Evil marks the horror director as 3-for-3 in my book. The contained yet still incredibly teerrifying story story follows a medial courier (Austin Abrams) on the night that a violent virus turns everyone into crazed, mutated killers. While the sci-fi/horror film definitely packs in the scares, I was still surprised how funny and accurate the film was to its video game source material. Among all the video game adaptation over the last few years, Resident Evil felt the most like actually playing the game.

One of the biggest movies of 2026 is, without question, Project Hail Mary. Based on the 2021 novel by Andy Weir, the smash hit follows a middle school science teacher (Ryan Gosling) who awakes with amnesia aboard a spacecraft light-years away from Earth. The newest movie from comedy giants Phil Lord and Christopher Miller was the right movie at the right time, its release accidentally coinciding with the Artemis II mission to the moon. That it was still playing in theaters more than a month after its release—an eternity in today’s theatrical landscape—speaks volumes about its out-of-this-world appeal.

The man who invented the modern summer blockbuster came back to theaters in 2026 with his first sci-fi in years: Disclosure Day. Emily Blunt and Josh O’Connor costar as two people who try to reveal the existence of extraterrestrial life, while the government tries to suppress the truth. It may not have been the glorious throwback to the days of E.T. and Close Encounters, but Disclosure Day gets by on career-best work by Blunt and another immaculate score by John Williams.

Alan Ritchson flexes his leading-man muscles in War Machine, a Netflix original that’s actually kinda worth watching. The Reacher star leads the movie as a staff sergeant for the Army Rangers who encounters a massively overpowered alien force during a training exercise. Sure, you’ve seen movies like this before—Predator, anyone?—but this throwback action flick is the best kind of turn-your-brain-off junk food we secretly crave.

You don’t have to be an overgrown ’80s baby to dig Masters of the Universe. This deeply affectionate reboot of the Reagan-era toy and TV franchise once again tells the story of Prince Adam (Nicholas Galitzine), who must save his fallen home of Eternia from the evil Skeletor (Jared Leto). The franchise started out as a cheap competitor to Star Wars, but the 2026 reboot is anything but a pale copy, with its dazzling visual design, snappy humor, and, most of all, a moving story about reasserting one’s place in the world.

How much you want to consider Marvel superhero movies “sci-fi” depends on you, but we safely consider radioactive spiders, mutant telepaths, and gamma-infused monsters to be firmly in the realm of science fiction. The fourth Tom Holland–led Spider-Man movie sees the web slinger working on his own until an invisible menace with the power to hijack people’s consciousness threatens everyone he still loves. It’s a box office (Hulk) smash, but Spider-Man: Brand New Day remembers that the true story lies in the hearts of heroes.

If Guy Ritchie made Looper, it might look like Mike & Nick & Nick & Alice. Emerging out of SXSW, the Hulu exclusive follows a gangster (Vince Vaughn) who, after regretting murdering his friend (James Marsden), uses a time machine to stop his past self. There are an awful lot of action comedies nowadays, but Mike & Nick & Nick & Alice stands out from the pack with some novel twists on well-worn time-travel formulas.

Sydney Chandler (Alien: Earth) and Takehiro Hira (Giri/Haji) hit the road in this tender and emotional sci-fi road trip from first-time director Brian Tetsuro Ivie. Set in a speculative near future in which one’s consciousness can be uploaded into the cloud, Anima sees Chandler star as an unemployed engineer who drives a wealthy and terminally ill man (Hira) across Connecticut in the hopes of finding his estranged son before the end of his life. Anima premiered to rave reviews at SXSW earlier this year and may soon find wide-release distribution.

Animated children’s films about woodland creatures are usually a mixed bag. For every Bambi or The Wild Robot, you’ll find a dozen films trying to become the next Over the Hedge. Hoppers dodges the repetitive trend by magically turning a young girl into a woodland creature, and the box-office numbers prove that the gimmick has paid off so far. The film follows Mabel Tanaka, who transports her consciousness into a robot beaver. She wants to rally the animals to protect their wilderness from becoming a highway. It’s a crazy idea. But considering the way we’re headed in 2026, I’m down for anything.

This SXSW darling stars Lewis Pullman and Maya Hawke as a couple with the ability to literally shape their environment. After attending a couples-counseling session run by a set of magic wonder twins, the two on-again, off-again lovers learn that their emotions have dire effects on the world around them. The silly, video-game-like premise isn’t Wishful Thinking’s only draw, however. At its heart, director Graham Parkes’s feature debut really touches on a question we all ask ourselves. How do we change for the person we love without sacrificing what makes us who we are?

Ralph Fiennes’s Dr. Ian Kelson experiments with some real science fiction in 28 Years Later: The Bone Temple, which is one of the first zombie flicks I’ve ever seen to reckon with the idea that a cure is physically attainable. This second entry in a planned sequel trilogy to the original 28 Days Later film also features Jack O’Connell (Sinners) as a maniacal cult leader styled after Jimmy Savile—just tearing up the apocalyptic landscape with Danny Boyle’s zany, violent humor.

The Mandalorian Officially Returns With a New Release

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

The Mandalorian and Grogu ushered in a return of Star Wars to the big screen. The Jon Favreau film expanded the hit Disney+ series The Mandalorian in a theatrical release and now you can bring the magic of the film home. Often, Star Wars brings fans plenty of books to love within the franchise. One of their most beloved series comes from Phillip Szostak and now fans can own their own version of The Art of Star Wars: The Mandalorian and Grogu! Based on the art that helped create The Mandalorian and Grogu, the book includes illustrations of characters like Zeb Orrelios and Grogu, as well as a look at the impressive opening sequence that featured Mando (Pedro Pascal) taking out multiple AT-AT walkers!

The Art of Star Wars books often take us deeper into the world and how some of our favorite films came to life. With the book inspired by The Mandalorian and Grogu, it marks the first theatrical release for Star Wars in seven years! The Mandalorian and Grogu follows Din Djarin (Pascal) as he embarks on a journey throughout the galaxy with his adopted son, Grogu. But when Mando falls in with the Hutts as he attempts to find Rotta the Hutt (Jeremy Allen White), Grogu is tested on just how much he is able to do on his own without his father by his side. Including befriending a bunch of Anzellans.

You can read more about the book here: "Filled with concept art, sketches, and interviews with key cast, crew, and creatives, The Art of Star Wars: The Mandalorian and Grogu provides readers with an exclusive look at the art created for the newest Star Wars film and the stories behind its characters, locations, and vehicles. The film stars Pedro Pascal, Sigourney Weaver, and Jeremy Allen White."

Four ships, four completely different reasons to be up there at all. Here is the one that has your seat, what flying with them would cost you, and what it would give back.

You belong on the fastest piece of junk in the galaxy, with a crew that took the job for the payday and then quietly stopped caring about the payday. Nothing on the Falcon has ever gone according to plan, and everybody aboard prefers it that way.

You belong with the crew that turned a tiny cell of misfits into an actual rebellion. The Ghost is a home before it is a ship, and everybody who boards it arrived with something broken that the rest of them decided to look after.

You belong with the four kids who found a buried ship, launched it by accident and are now extremely lost in a galaxy that eats people like them. Nobody here knows what they are doing, and that turns out to be the fun of it.

You belong in the quietest ship in the galaxy, with one small passenger and a code you have never explained to anybody. You told yourself this was a job, and then it stopped being a job somewhere around the second week.

The Mandalorian kicked Disney+ off to an exciting start as the first series on the platform. Since, it has remained one of the most beloved Star Wars series prior to The Mandalorian and Grogu's release. People absolutely love watching Grogu waddle around and now that he's growing more into his powers, he's acting more independent! It's what made The Mandalorian and Grogu so fun to watch. While The Art of Star Wars: The Mandalorian and Grogu isn't giving us a new adventure with Din and Grogu, it is a deeper look into the franchise and that's amazing.

You can see the Mandalorian and his sweet son Grogu in the film The Mandalorian and Grogu.

The Mandalorian & Grogu follows Din Djarin (The Mandalorian) and his adopted son, Grogu, as they embark on a new adventure. After settling on the planet Nevarro at the end of Season 3, their peaceful life is disrupted, leading them back into the galaxy's conflicts.

Princess Kate Says Nature Helped Her Heal After Her Cancer Diagnosis

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

“People have long sought comfort in the natural world”

It’s not very often that Princess Catherine sits down for a traditional magazine interview, but in a new story published today in Country Life, the Princess of Wales got candid about her recovery following her 2024 cancer diagnosis and subsequent chemotherapy treatment (she went into remission early last year).

“I was told in hospital that my recovery would not be linear,” Kate says. “[That] there will be good days and bad days, that it will take time.”

She adds: “What is interesting is to see the science now back up what we instinctively know and feel. Humans have always turned to nature. Whether it is the beach for healing and recovery, the countryside for peace and perspective or the mountains to find solitude, people have long sought comfort in the natural world. It is such a restorative environment and incorporating nature into someone’s recovery pathway is vital.”

This past June, Kate completed the National Three Peaks Challenge, a feat that involves climbing the three highest peaks in the United Kingdom in a matter of only 24 hours. Part of the reason she took on the challenge was to highlight “the deeper impact of serious illness and the importance of holistic healthcare.”

The royal has also been teaming up with organizations that focus on outdoor activity and mental health, like Mind Over Mountains and the Dose of Nature charity.

In the wake of these events, interviewer Robert Macfarlane spoke with Kate about how nature tied to her recovery process. Of her “good days and bad days,” Kate noted that nature demonstrates the same ebb and flow. She said, “It shows us that periods of rest, reflection, and renewal are equally as important as moments of growth.”

Kate also spoke about her longstanding love of the outdoors. “I remember from a very early age spending a lot of time in nature, playing in streams, walking in the countryside, blackberry picking or simply time alone in my own world, enjoying the simplicity of ordinary things, such as digging under the laurel bush for what I thought was treasure,” she said. “There was always a sense of freedom I felt in nature, something about being outdoors that is hard to find elsewhere.”

She went on to note how, as adults, we often lose this sense of wonderment, and she hopes to instill the same fascination with the natural world in her three kids: Prince George, 13, Princess Charlotte, 11, and Prince Louis, 8. “We are often so busy we simply forget to pause, slow down and notice the detail in life,” Kate said. “It is something I remind myself to do with my own children; to give them freedom to explore, rather than constantly telling them what to see or what to think.”

Russia-Ukraine talks turn to post-war security but end without breakthrough

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

On Thursday Steve Witkoff and Jared Kushner will travel to Moscow and Dublin to hold talks with the Russian and Ukrainian presidents

Trilateral talks in Abu Dhabi between Russia, Ukraine and the US aimed at ending the war in Ukraine have concluded after a second day.

Although the American side said they saw "some openings", there was no hint of any breakthroughs to end the nearly five year-long war sparked by Russia's invasion.

However, both Moscow and Kyiv confirmed that a large-scale prisoner of war exchange was in the works and could be carried out soon.

Davyd Arakhamia from Ukraine's negotiating team said that this round of talks - the first since February - "was one of the hardest."

"I believe the Russians came with very strict directives, so it was extremely difficult to soften the discussion," he said. But he added that he felt "good momentum" and urged for a second round of talks to be organised as soon as possible.

Comments by Kremlin negotiator Vladimir Medinsky were less conciliatory. "Right now... a lot depends on Ukraine, on the position of the US, and on whether Europe comes to its senses," he said.

After the talks it was announced that on Thursday US negotiators Steve Witkoff and Jared Kushner - President Donald Trump's son-in-law - would travel to Moscow to meet Russian President Vladimir Putin, and then to Dublin to speak to Ukraine's Volodymyr Zelensky.

The meetings in Abu Dhabi had been framed as centred around the "security architecture" of Europe.

For Kyiv, this may include reassurances that allies will come to its aid if Ukraine comes under attack again, despite the country not being a Nato member.

For Moscow, a reworked "security framework" is likely to include a limit to the influence of Nato in eastern Europe - a central concern for the Kremlin since the alliance's eastward expansion.

Earlier this week Kushner said the US hoped to "create a new framework" of security between Ukraine, Russia and Europe that could "secure lasting peace", while the Kremlin's Medinsky said the world needed a "new" and "fair" security system.

That framework would serve as "a new Yalta and Potsdam", Medinsky said, referring to the 1940s summits in which the victorious Allied powers carved up post-war Europe into spheres of influence.

Ukraine and Europe have long been concerned about the current US administration's commitment to European security and its softening towards the Kremlin.

Representatives of Britain, Nato and various EU countries were also in Abu Dhabi and met with US and Ukrainian negotiators, but the Kremlin - with whom the EU does not officially communicate - said its delegation had not had any contact with them.

Moscow has been dismissive of European involvement, although Europeans have long argued that they are central to any post-war dynamics on their continent.

Many - particularly in eastern Europe - feel their security is linked to that of Ukraine. Agreements cannot be implemented "if no consensus can be reached among Nato members," a German government spokesperson said on Wednesday.

Kushner said the US was keen to bring the Europeans on board and said that getting them "added to the format" would be on the agenda for his talks with Putin.

There is also disagreement on whether discussions on a future security framework should be preceded by a ceasefire, as the Europeans and Kyiv would want, or whether the fighting only stops after an agreement has been reached, as the Kremlin has insisted.

Any agreement on a peacetime security framework, however, is contingent on the war ending, Kushner noted. But that cannot occur "until there's an agreement on territory and on all other bilateral issues," he added.

Since talks began last year, a core obstacle to any settlement has been Ukraine's eastern region of Donetsk, which is mostly occupied by Russia. Kyiv wants to freeze the contact line to maintain control over those areas, which it says are essential to its security.

Moscow, meanwhile, has never backed down from its demand that Ukraine relinquish the parts of Donetsk it still holds. "Every inch" of land would be "returned to Russia one way or another", Medinsky said.

Kushner acknowledged that the territorial question remained the hardest, while Zelensky's chief of staff and negotiator Kyrylo Budanov refused to be drawn on whether Russia was still demanding a Ukrainian withdrawal from the region.

Later, Budanov admitted the talks had been "tough" and that Ukraine's priority was to ensure Russia could not attack it again.

"These deals are very hard and complex, and this one is very emotional, very complex," Kushner said. "The reality is there is no trust right now between the sides and that is to be expected after a conflict like this."

‘Ozark’ Star’s Madonna Biopic Finally Gets an Update

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Earlier this year, it was proven beyond a doubt that audience desire for more musical biopics hasn't faded. Antoine Fuqua’s musical biopic, Michael, all about the King of Pop's rise to the spotlight and his early career struggles, became the first biopic of all time to hit $1 billion in theaters in an impressive theatrical run. This off-the-wall success came courtesy of an audience hungry to experience Jackson's best songs on the big screen, despite all of the negative reviews that the movie was met with.

With a four-part Beatles biopic on the way and Michael in the rearview mirror, it's high time that the biggest stars in music past and present were represented on the big screen. One musical biopic that's been waiting in the wings since the start of the decade is Madonna, which was first announced in 2020. However, the project has since been in development limbo, as it was shelved at Universal early last year, despite Madonna herself being attached to both pen the script and direct the film.

Now, Weapons favorite Julia Garner, who is still technically attached to play the titular pop icon, has offered the latest update on whether the biopic might finally get made. Speaking with CBS, she admitted that "Every hour it changes, in a way," when asked about the biopic's current status. It’s not up to me. I would love for the movie to get made, obviously. But I also feel whatever happens, I made a great friend," she added. "I love Madonna. I think she’s incredible." These quotes come a year on from Garner telling a podcast that the movie is "supposed to still happen."

Four worlds and four completely different ideas of what a good life looks like. This is where you belong, what it would ask of you, and what you would get back in return.

You belong somewhere old enough that the mountains have names and somebody can still tell you who gave them. Middle-earth is for people who think the right thing is worth doing even when it will not be finished in their lifetime.

You want the sea, a crew, and absolutely nobody telling you where you are allowed to go. The Grand Line is the world for people whose main ambition in life is finding out what is over there.

You would rather be feared and useful than liked and useless. The Continent is a world where the monsters are the straightforward part of the job and the people are where it all gets complicated.

You want the myths to be real and loose in the actual world, and you want to find out at about thirteen that you were never anywhere near as ordinary as everybody kept telling you.

Albeit with much less fanfare than the aforementioned Michael, a brand-new biopic of the legendary Jimmy Stewart, simply titled Jimmy, has finally hit theaters. Alas, its opening weekend proved a struggle, as Jimmy scored just over $2 million in domestic ticket sales from 1,467 locations. Riverdale star KJ Apa leads the film as the It's a Wonderful Life icon, alongside the likes of Jason Alexander, Neal McDonough, and Rob Riggle.

You can stream Michael on Starz. For the latest updates, stay tuned to Collider.
